]
.

(i)

It follows immediately that there are 15
\mathbf{W}
-MASS’s
of seven types as below:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\{X\otimes I,I\otimes X,X\otimes X\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{Y}

\displaystyle=\left\{Y\otimes I,I\otimes Y,Y\otimes Y\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{Z}

\displaystyle=\left\{Z\otimes I,I\otimes Z,Z\otimes Z\right\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\{X\otimes X,Y\otimes Z,Z\otimes Y\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{Y}

\displaystyle=\left\{Y\otimes Y,Z\otimes X,X\otimes Z\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{Z}

\displaystyle=\left\{Z\otimes Z,X\otimes Y,Y\otimes X\right\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{YZ}

\displaystyle=\left\{Y\otimes I,I\otimes Z,Y\otimes Z\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{ZX}

\displaystyle=\left\{Z\otimes I,I\otimes X,Z\otimes X\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{XY}

\displaystyle=\left\{X\otimes I,I\otimes Y,X\otimes Y\right\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{YZ}

\displaystyle=\left\{I\otimes Y,Z\otimes I,Z\otimes Y\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{ZX}

\displaystyle=\left\{I\otimes Z,X\otimes I,X\otimes Z\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{XY}

\displaystyle=\left\{I\otimes X,Y\otimes I,Y\otimes X\right\};

\displaystyle\text{together with}\quad\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=\left\{X\otimes X,Y\otimes Y,Z\otimes Z\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=\left\{X\otimes Y,Y\otimes Z,Z\otimes X\right\},

\displaystyle\mbox{and,}\quad\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle=\left\{Y\otimes X,Z\otimes Y,X\otimes Z\right\}.

(3.15)

(ii)

Each unitary in
\mathbf{W}
occurs in exactly three of them. Figure 5
gives an idea.

Figure 5.

(iii)

The figure facilitates
a pictorial understanding of the contents of
[
31
]
as well. We elaborate in words
as follows.

(a)

Three navy blue circles put together overlap with the remaining subsystems.

(b)

Three maroon lines put together overlap with the remaining subsystems.

(c)

Three yellow quadrilaterals put together can be combined with the middle and the outer circle.
But, they overlap with green and sky-blue on the hexagon and also with maroon and navy-blue on the inner circle.

(d)

Three sky-blue quadrilaterals put together can be combined with the middle and the inner circle.
But, they overlap with green and yellow on the hexagon and also with maroon and navy-blue on the outer circle.

(e)

Three green triangles put together can be combined with the inner and the outer circle.
But, they overlap with yellow and sky-blue on the hexagon and also with maroon and navy-blue on the middle circle.

The first sentence in each part of (c), (d), (e) gives an MUB.

3.3.
Positive operator-valued measures

Theoretically speaking, if
\mathbf{U}
is a unitary basis then the tuple

({\rm tr}\,\,(\rho U_{x}))_{x\in X}
determines the state
\rho.
The same is true
if we take any unitary system
\mathbf{W}=\{W_{y}:y\in Y\}
of size
(d^{2}-1)
and consider the
tuple
({\rm tr}\,\,(\rho W_{y}))_{y\in Y}
as a representative of
\rho.

But usually such measurements are performed with respect to a set of projections or positive
operators, the so-called, positive-operator-valued measure (POVM) viz., a set
\mathbf{A}=\{A_{j}:1\leq j\leq v\}
of positive operators on
\mathcal{H}
with
\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\textit{v}}A_{j}=I
. For computational convenience, these
A_{j}
’s are taken to be suitable positive multiples of rank one projections except possibly for one, because we may
take the remaining one to make the sum equal to
I
. Such POVM’s are called pure POVM’s.
Alternatively, we may consider an informationally complete system of rank one projections,
say
\{P_{j},1\leq j\leq\nu\}
, such that

\left(\text{tr}\rho P_{j})\right)_{j=1}^{\nu}
determine the state
\rho
. And the aim will remain to have
\nu
the ideal number
d^{2}
,
or, an optimal number by a specific method. Whenever possible, the method should also ensure that the number of
quantum mechanical overlaps,
\text{tr}(P_{j}P_{k}),1\leq j\neq k\leq\nu
is small as well.

3.3.1.

We draw upon
[
3
]
, particularly excerpts given in Remark
3.1.2
(iv).

(i)

If
\mathbf{W}
can be partitioned as union of
(d+1)

\mathbf{W}
-MASS’s, say
\{\mathbf{V}_{s}:0\leq s\leq d\}
of size

(d-1),
then the complete system of
(d+1)
orthonormal bases, say,
\left\{\{\mathbf{b}_{t}^{s}:0\leq t\leq d-1\},0\leq s\leq d\right\},
of

\mathbb{C}^{d}
obtained via [3], Theorem 3.2 (viz.,
\{\mathbf{b}_{t}^{s}:0\leq t\leq d-1\}
is a common orthonormal eigenbasis for
\mathbf{\mathbf{V}_{s}},

0\leq s\leq d
) gives rise to
(d^{2}-1)
pure states

\{\rho_{j}:1\leq j\leq d^{2}-1\}
with
\rho_{s(d-1)+t}
determined by the
unit vector
\mathbf{b}_{t}^{s},

1\leq t\leq d-1,

0\leq s\leq d.
Thus we
obtain a pure POVM
\{A_{j}=\frac{1}{(d+1)}\rho_{j},\,\,0\leq j\leq d^{2}-1\},
where
\rho_{0}=(d+1)I-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d{{}^{2}}-1}\rho_{j}.
See also Remark
3.1.2
(iv).

(ii)

As noted in Theorem
3.2.1
(ii) above, if each
W_{y}
in
\mathbf{W}
has
simple eigenvalues, then
\mathbf{V}_{\alpha}
’s constituting the fan
representation of
\mathbf{W}
are mutually disjoint. This is a situation
similar to (i) above except that there is no guarantee that the sizes of

\mathbf{V}_{\alpha}
’s are all
(d-1)
or, equivalently, that of
\Lambda
is

(d+1).
We do not yet have an example for that. In any case, the technique
indicated in (i) above does give a pure POVM of size
(d-1)\,\#\Lambda+1.
Rewording, the Constrained Technique 3.1.2
for the trivial constraint gives an informationally complete system of
(d-1)\#\Lambda+1
rank one projections.

(iii)

In general, for a composite
d
which is not a prime power, or, even when

d
is a prime power, a given
\mathbf{W}
may not be decomposable as in (i)
above. This can be seen in
Figures 3 and 4 for Example
3.2.5
and Figure 5 for Example
3.2.6
.
Figure 4 makes it clear that the whole fan is needed to cover
\mathbf{W}

and in Figure 3, only the red part can be ignored to obtain a smaller subset of the
fan to cover
\mathbf{W}
. Once again, Theorem
3.2.1
, Remark
3.2.2
(ii) and Example
3.1.3
tell us that
overlapping
W_{y}
’s have to possess some multiple eigenvalues and, therefore,
their spectral projection sets are all constraints. In the rest of this
section we make an attempt to obtain pure POVM’s or informationally complete systems of rank one projections of optimal size for such
situations.

3.3.2.

What comes in handy for our purpose is a minimal subset, say,

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{W}},
of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathbf{W}}
satisfying

\mathbf{W}=\cup\{\mathbf{V}:\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{W}}\}.
We may write

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{W}}=\{\mathbf{V}_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\Lambda_{1}\}
with
\Lambda_{1}\subset\Lambda,
if we like.

(i)

§3.3.1 above, particularly, part (iii) throws some light on it together with its size and
various subsections of §2 tell the general story
for some special
\mathbf{W}
. The smallest general one is, of course, as given
in §2.2.4. In the context of Example taken up in §2.3 to §2.5, the next one is as in §2.5.
We emphasize that the
\mathbf{W}
-MASS’s coming from cyclic subgroups only are needed for the corresponding
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{W}}
.

(ii)

A crude way to obtain a pure POVM would be to consider a common
orthonormal eigenbasis
\{{\bf b}_{t}^{\alpha}:0\leq t\leq d-1\}
for
{\bf V}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\Lambda_{1}
and construct
a pure POVM as in (i) above, say
{\bf A}=\{A_{j}:0\leq j\leq(d-1)\#\Lambda_{1}\}
. We can
refine this construction to obtain a pure POVM of smaller size.

3.4.
Utilization of the techniques in §3.1 to obtain an optimal measurement set

As noted in 3.3.1 (iii), overlapping
W_{y}
’s
have to possess some multiple eigenvalues. The projections on the corresponding
eigenspaces can be taken to be
Q_{t}
’s
giving rise to a constraint
\mathbf{Q}
. In the rest of this section
we make an attempt to obtain pure POVM’s of optimal size for such situations
using our technique outlined in §3.1.

3.4.1.
Reorganization of elementary measurements for
{\bf W}
- MASS’s

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\bf W}=\{{\bf V}_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\Lambda_{1}\}
be a minimal system as in 3.3.2 above.

(i)

Let
Y_{1}=\{y\in Y:{\bf W}_{y}\in~\text{a unique}~{\bf V}_{\alpha}~\text{with}~\alpha\in\Lambda_{1}\}
and
Y_{2}=Y\setminus Y_{1}
=

\{y\in Y:{\bf W}_{y}\in{\bf V}_{\alpha}\bigcap{\bf V}_{\alpha^{\prime}}~\text{ for
some}~\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime}~\text{in}~\Lambda_{1}\}
. The case
Y_{2}=\emptyset
has been considered in 3.3.1 (ii).
We consider the case
Y_{2}\neq\emptyset
.
Then each
y\in Y_{2}
determines a costraint, say,
{\bf Q}^{{\bf W}_{y}}
, viz., the set of eigenprojections of
{\bf W}_{y}
with distinct eigenvalues.
Different
y
’s in
Y_{2}
may determine the same constraint as we will see in §3.5 and §3.6; for all such
y
’s, the corresponding
W_{y}
’s commute.
Let
\Lambda_{2}
=

\{\alpha\in\Lambda_{1}:{\bf V}_{\alpha}~\text{contains a}~{\bf W}_{y}~\text{for some}~y\in Y_{2}\}
,

\Lambda_{3}
=
\{\alpha\in\Lambda_{1}:{\bf V}_{\alpha}~\text{consists of}~{\bf W}_{y}\text{'s}~\text{with}~y\text{'s}\in Y_{1}\}=\Lambda_{1}\setminus\Lambda_{2}
.

(ii)

We note that for Example
3.2.5
(a) above
\#\Lambda_{1}=6
by taking the first six
{\bf W}
-MASS’s,
\#Y=15
,
\#Y_{1}=12
,
\#Y_{2}=3,\Lambda_{3}=\emptyset
(see Figure 3). But, for Example
3.2.5
(b),
\Lambda=\Lambda_{1}

with
\#\Lambda=12,\#Y=35
,
\#Y_{1}=24
,
\#Y_{2}=11,\Lambda_{3}=\emptyset
(see Figure 4). On the other hand, for Example 4.4 (v)(e),
\#Y=35
,

\#\Lambda_{1}=22
,
\#Y_{1}=34
,
\#Y_{2}=1
,
\#\Lambda_{2}=4
,

\#\Lambda_{3}=18
(see Figure 17 in §4).

(iii)

For
\alpha\in\Lambda_{1}
, there is a complete common unit eigenvector system
for
W_{y}
’s in
{\bf V}_{\alpha}
and thus the set
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}
of corresponding rank one projections, say,
P_{\alpha,j},0\leq j\leq q-1
.

(iv)

We now collect distinct constraints
{\bf Q}^{{\bf W}_{y}}
with
y
in
Y_{2}
, say

\{{\bf Q}^{(s)}:1\leq s\leq\nu\}
.
Suppose
\alpha\in\Lambda_{2}
. Then there is a
y\in Y_{2}
with
{\bf W}_{y}
belonging to
{\bf V}_{\alpha}
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}
is

{\bf Q}^{{\bf W}_{y}}
-constrained.
Also
{\bf Q}^{{\bf W}_{y}}=Q^{(s)}
for some
1\leq s\leq\nu
. If
{\bf Q}^{{\bf W}_{z}}={\bf Q}^{(s)}
for some
z\in Y_{2}
, then, in view of (iii) above,
W_{z}

commutes with each
W_{t}
in
{\bf V}_{\alpha}
. So by maximality of
{\bf V}_{\alpha}
, we have that
{\bf W}_{z}\in{\bf V}_{\alpha}
. Therefore, we may talk in
terms of constraints
Q^{(s)},1\leq s\leq\nu
only.

(v)

For
1\leq s\leq\nu
, let
\mathcal{P}^{(s)}=\{\mathcal{P}_{\beta}:\mathcal{P}_{\beta}~\text{is}~Q^{(s)}~\text{constrained}\}
=
\{\mathcal{P}_{v}^{s}:1\leq v\leq g_{s}\}
, say. Then

\cup_{1\leq s\leq\nu}\mathcal{P}^{(s)}=\{\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\Lambda_{2}\}
. Hence
\{\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\Lambda_{1}\}
=

\{\mathcal{P}_{v}^{s}:1\leq s\leq\nu,1\leq v\leq g_{s}\}\cup\{\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\Lambda_{3}\}
and for
1\leq s\leq\nu
,
\mathcal{P}_{v}^{s}
is

{\bf Q}^{(s)}
-constrained for
1\leq v\leq g_{s}
.

(vi)

We note that the above reorganization leaves the scope of

(a)

overlaps amongst
\{\mathcal{P}^{(s)},1\leq s\leq\nu\}
and
\{\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\Lambda_{3}\}
,

(b)

overlaps amongst

\{P_{s,v,j}:0\leq j\leq q-1\},1\leq s\leq\nu,1\leq v\leq g_{s}
, and also

\{P_{\alpha,j}:0\leq j\leq q-1\},\alpha\in\Lambda_{3}
.

But, all the same, we note below an application of this and Theorem
3.1.4
for an attempt to
reduce the number of rank one projections as opposed to the crude limit presented in 3.3.2 (ii).

Theorem 3.4.1
.

For estimation purposes the collection

\Pi=\{P_{\alpha,j}:\alpha\in\Lambda_{1},0\leq j\leq q-1\}
can be replaced by a smaller collection
\Pi^{\prime}
with

\#\Pi^{\prime}\leq(\sum_{s=1}^{\nu}g_{s}+\#\Lambda_{3})q-\#\Lambda_{3}-\sum_{s=1}^{\nu}(g_{s}-1)\tau_{s}-(\nu-1)
.

Proof.

If
\Lambda_{3}=\emptyset
, then the result coincides with Theorem
3.1.4
.
The only extra point needed to be noted is that if
\#\Lambda_{3}\geq 1
, then we
may take anchor as any
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\Lambda_{3}
. So
\{\mathcal{P}_{\alpha},\alpha\in\Lambda_{3}\}
require

q+(\#\Lambda_{3}-1)(q-1)
rank one projections and we may ignore one more
projection while applying Theorem
3.1.4
.
∎

3.4.2.
Simultaneous Constraints

We can apply the Simultaneous Constraints Technique 3.1.3 in case certain
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}
’s have more than one constraint and
reduce the number further. But the general case will involve two much of mathematical jargon. We will only illusrate this in §3.6.

3.5.
Some useful properties for the cyclic group case of shift and multiply (Schwinger Basis) as in §2.3

3.5.1.

We begin with the relationships between the unitary operators in the unitary basis. It will help to keep in view related examples
above as well.

Proposition 3.5.1
.

In the context of §2.3 and Example
3.2.5
, for
m,n,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
, we have the following facts.

(i)

U_{jm,jn}=(\eta_{d})^{-\frac{j(j-1)}{2}mn}(U_{m,n})^{j}
.

(ii)

U_{m,n}^{d}=\pm I_{d}
, it is
I_{d}
if and only if
mn(d-1)
is even.

(iii)

If
\lambda_{(m,n)}
is of full size, then
U_{(m,n)}
has simple eigenvalues.

Proof.

(i) It is trivially true for
j=0
or
(m,n)=(0,0)
because
U_{0,0}=I_{d}
. Now suppose
j\neq 0
. Consider any
(m,n)\neq(0,0)
in

\mathbb{Z}_{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{d}
. Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
. Then
U_{jm,jn}|k\rangle=(\eta_{d})^{jmk}|(jn+k)\rangle
. Also,

\displaystyle U_{m,n}|k\rangle

\displaystyle=(\eta_{d})^{mk}|(n+k)\rangle,~\text{and, therefore,}

\displaystyle U_{m,n}^{2}|k\rangle

\displaystyle=(\eta_{d})^{mk}(\eta_{d})^{m(n+k)}|(2n+k)\rangle=(\eta_{d})^{2mk}(\eta_{d})^{nm}|(2n+k)\rangle,

\displaystyle U_{m,n}^{3}|k\rangle

\displaystyle=(\eta_{d})^{mk}(\eta_{d})^{m(n+k)}(\eta_{d})^{m(2n+k)}|(3n+k)\rangle

\displaystyle=(\eta_{d})^{3mk}(\eta_{d})^{nm(1+2)}|(3n+k)\rangle.

(3.16)

Proceeding like this

\displaystyle U_{m,n}^{j}|k\rangle

\displaystyle=(\eta_{d})^{jmk}(\eta_{d})^{nm(1+2+\cdots+(j-1))}|(jn+k)\rangle

\displaystyle=(\eta_{d})^{jmk}(\eta_{d})^{nm(\frac{j(j-1)}{2}}|(jn+k)\rangle.

(3.17)

Hence (i) is true.

As to (ii), from the proof of (i) for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
,

\displaystyle U_{m,n}^{(d-1)}|k\rangle=\eta_{d}^{(d-1)mn}\eta_{d}^{mn(d-2)(d-1)/2}|(d-1)n+k\rangle,

(3.18)

and, therefore

\displaystyle U_{m,n}^{(d)}|k\rangle

\displaystyle=\eta_{d}^{(d-1)mk}\eta_{d}^{mn(d-2)(d-1)/2}\eta_{d}^{m((d-1)n+k)}|k\rangle=\eta_{d}^{mnd(d-1)/2}|k\rangle,

\displaystyle=e^{\pi imn(d-1)}|k\rangle.

(3.19)

Hence (ii) follows.

Turning to (iii), note that
\{U_{(m,n)},m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}\}
are linearly independent. But by (i),
U_{(jm,jn)}=(\eta_{d})^{-\frac{j(j-1)}{2}mm}U_{(m,n)}^{j}
for
0\leq j,m,n\leq d-1
. So, for
(m,n)
with
\lambda_{(m,n)}
of full size,
\{U_{(jm,jn)};0\leq j\leq d-1\}
are linearly
independent and, therefore
\{U_{(m,n)}^{j};0\leq j\leq d-1\}
are linearly independent. Consider any such
(m,n)
. If
U_{(m,n)}
has a multiple
eigenvalue, say
\lambda_{1}
, with eigenprojection
S_{1}
of dimension
\geq 2
, then
U_{(m,n)}=\sum_{k=1}^{s}\lambda_{k}S_{k}
with
2\leq s<d
with

\lambda_{k}
’s all distinct and
S_{k}
’s mutually orthogonal projections with
0\neq S_{k}\neq I_{d}
. So

\{U_{(m,n)}^{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{s}\lambda_{k}^{j}S_{k}:0\leq j\leq d-1\}
is contained in the span of
s
linearly independent projections
S_{k}
’s, which
has dimension
s<d
, a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 3.5.2
.

Let
m,n,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.

(i)

Eigenvalues of
U_{m,n}
are in the set
\{\eta_{d}^{s}:s\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}\}
or
e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{d}\right)}\{\eta_{d}^{s}:s\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}\}
according
as
mn(d-1)
is even or odd.

(ii)

Eigenspaces of
U_{jm,jn}
and
U_{m,n}^{j}
are the same.

Proof.

It is immediate from Proposition
3.5.1
or its proof above.
∎

Theorem 3.5.3
.

Let
d
be a composite number and
j
a factor of
d
other than
1
and
d
. Set
u=d/j
. Let
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.

(i)

The set
{\bf Q}
of the eigenprojections of
U_{jm,jn}
is a constraint.

(ii)

For any orthonormal system
\{\xi_{k}:0\leq k\leq d-1\}
of eigenvectors of
U_{m,n}
,
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{k}=|\xi_{k}\rangle\langle\xi_{k}|:0\leq k\leq d-1\}
is
{\bf Q}
-constrained.

(iii)

For
m^{\prime},n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}
with
m^{\prime}\equiv m~(\text{mod}~u)
and
n^{\prime}\equiv n~(\text{mod}~u)
, any orthonormal
system
\{\xi_{k}^{\prime}:0\leq k\leq d-1\}
of eigenvectors of
U_{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\{P_{k}^{\prime}=|\xi_{k}^{\prime}\rangle\langle\xi_{k}^{\prime}|,0\leq k\leq d-1\}
is
{\bf Q}
-constrained.

(iv)

Theorem 3.3 can be applied to reduce the number of rank one projections obtained from a minimal
\mathcal{M}_{\bf W}
for
estimation purposes.

Proof.

If
U_{m,n}
has multiple eigenvalues, let
\lambda
be such an eigenvlue with multiplicity
v
. Then
\lambda^{j}
is an eigenvalue
of
U_{m,n}^{j}
of multiplicty at least
v
. So using Corollary
3.5.2
(ii)
{\bf Q}
is a constraint. It is clearly, a constraint for
\mathcal{P}

by Spectral Mapping Theorem applied to powers of
U_{m,n}
.

Now suppose
U_{m,n}
has only simple eigenvalues. We have
d=ju
with
u\geq 2
.
For
0\leq v\leq j-1
,
0\leq s\leq u-1
,
(\eta_{d}^{s+vu})^{j}=\eta_{d}^{sj}
. So
U_{m,n}^{j}
has eigenvalues
\{\eta_{d}^{sj}:0\leq s\leq u-1\}

or
\{e^{\left(\frac{j\pi i}{d}\right)}\eta_{d}^{sj}:0\leq s\leq u-1\}
each of multiplicity
j
according as
mn(d-1)
is even or odd.

So using Corollary
3.5.2
above,
{\bf Q}
is a uniform constraint and
\mathcal{P}
is
{\bf Q}
-constrained. Hence both (i) and (ii) hold.

As to (iii) We have only to note that in this case
jm^{\prime}\equiv jm~(\text{mod}~d)
and
jn^{\prime}\equiv jn~(\text{mod}~d)
.

Turning to (iv), consider any
\sigma=(m,n)
with
\lambda_{\sigma}
of full size giving rise to a
{\bf W}
-MASS
{\bf V}_{\sigma}
. Then
for
\sigma^{\prime}=(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
with
m^{\prime}\equiv m~(\text{mod}~u)
and
n^{\prime}\equiv n~(\text{mod}~u)
,

{\bf V}_{\sigma}
and
{\bf V}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
overlap in
{\bf V}_{(jm,jn)}
for sure. We now appeal to the discussion in the beginning
of §3.4.
∎

Remark 3.5.4
.

Exact ideal number
d^{2}
can be obtained for the case
d=p^{2}
,
d=pa
with
p
and
a
both primes,
p<a
. This will
be taken up in §3.6.

3.5.2.

A practical approach to obtain elementary measurements for the case of composite
d
and
U_{m,n}
having
simple eigenvalues
. Let
j
be a factor of
d
other than
1
or
d
and
u=d/j
and other notation and terminology
also as in Theorem
3.5.3
above and its proof.

Remark 3.5.5
.

We explain the approach in different steps as follows.

Step (i)

It consists of first obtaining for
1\leq k\leq u
, eigenspaces
E_{k}
’s and any mutually orthogonal system of unit
eigenvectors in
E_{k}
, say
\{\xi_{k,v}:0\leq v\leq j-1\}
for
U_{jm,jn}
. We then set
Q_{k}=P_{E_{k}}
, the projection on
E_{k}
for

1\leq k\leq u
and
{\bf Q}=\{Q_{k}:1\leq k\leq u\}
.

Step (ii)

It consists of considering the restrictions
U_{m,n}
to
E_{k}
and obtaining a mutually orthogonal system of unit
eigenvectors for
U_{m,n}/E_{k}
, say
\{\eta_{k,\beta}:0\leq\beta\leq j-1\}
in terms of

\{\xi_{k,v}:0\leq v\leq j-1\}
and then pool them together varying
k
from
1
to
u
to form a complete
system of mutually orthogonal unit eigenvectors
\{\eta_{k,\beta}:1\leq k\leq u,0\leq\beta\leq j-1\}
for

U_{m,n}
and accordingly the set
\mathcal{P}
of rank one mutually orthogonal projections

\{P_{k,\beta}=|\eta_{k,\beta}\rangle\langle\eta_{k,\beta}|:1\leq k\leq u,0\leq\beta\leq j-1\}
for the
spectral representation of
U_{m,n}
. Clearly,
\sum_{\beta=0}^{j-1}P_{k,\beta}=Q_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq u
and
therefore,
\mathcal{P}
is
{\bf Q}
-constrained.

Step (iii)

is to note that for another
(0,0)\neq(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{d}
with
(jm^{\prime},jn^{\prime})=(jm,jn)
we
can repeat step (ii) with
(m,n)
replaced by
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
using
{\bf Q}
etc. as in (i), above. Thus we can
obtain various
\eta_{k,\beta}^{\prime}
,
P_{k,\beta}^{\prime}
accordingly,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\{P_{k,\beta}^{\prime}=|\eta_{k,\beta}^{\prime}\rangle\langle\eta_{k,\beta}^{\prime}|:1\leq k\leq u,0\leq\beta\leq j-1\}
which is

{\bf Q}
-constrained because
\sum_{\beta=0}^{j-1}P_{k,\beta}^{\prime}=Q_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq u
.

Step (iv)

Finally, we note that convenience lies in the fact that quantum mechanical overlaps for
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
are easy to obtain
in the sense that for
1\leq k\neq k^{\prime}\leq u,0\leq\beta,\beta^{\prime}\leq j-1
we have
P_{k,\beta}P_{k^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{\prime}=0

and, therefore,
\text{tr}(P_{k,\beta}P_{k^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}^{\prime})=0
. On the other hand, for
1\leq k\leq u,0\leq\beta,\beta^{\prime}\leq j-1
,
we have
\text{tr}(P_{k,\beta}P_{k,\beta^{\prime}}^{\prime})=|\langle\eta_{k,\beta}|\eta_{k,\beta^{\prime}}^{\prime}\rangle|^{2}=|\sum_{v=0}^{j-1}\overline{\eta_{k,\beta,v}}{\eta_{k,\beta^{\prime},v}^{\prime}}|^{2}
where
\eta_{k,\beta}=\sum_{v=0}^{j-1}\eta_{k,\beta,v}\xi_{k,v}
and

\eta_{k,\beta^{\prime}}^{\prime}=\sum_{v=0}^{j-1}\eta_{k,\beta^{\prime},v}^{\prime}\xi_{k,v}
.

We shall demonstrate all this numerically for the case
d=p^{2}
or
d=pa
with
p
and
a
distinct primes in §3.6 after a little
more preparation.

3.5.3.
Basic modular arithmetic

We consider the case
d=pa
with
p
,
a
prime,
p<a
.
There exist
j_{0},k_{0},r_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
with
r_{0}<p
such that
a=j_{0}p+r_{0}
and
p^{k_{0}}<a<p^{k_{0}+1}
.

Remark 3.5.6
.

(i)

The following observations from Modular Arithmetic will be helpful. We
write equalities modulo
d
unless otherwise specified.

(a)

For
n\in Z_{d}
with
\text{HCF}(n,d)=1
, there exists a unique
\hat{n}
with
n\hat{n}\equiv 1~(\text{mod}\,d)
.
As a consequence, for
m\in Z_{d}
,
(m,n)=n(m\hat{n},1)
.

(b)

For
1\leq r\leq p-1
,
\{{\rm tr}:1\leq t\leq p-1\}(\mathrm{mod}\,p)=\{j:1\leq j\leq p-1\}
and similarly, for
a
.

(ii)

By (i)(a) there are
1\leq s_{0}\leq p-1
and
u\geq 0
that satisfy
r_{0}s_{0}=up+1
, i.e.,
r_{0}s_{0}=1~(\text{mod}~p)
.

(a)

If
p
and
a
are both odd, then
p+2\leq a
. Therefore,
j_{0}
and
r_{0}
in
a=j_{0}p+r_{0}
satisfy atleast one of
j_{0}
and
r_{0}
is
\geq 2
. Also

s_{0}=1
if and only if
r_{0}=1
and therefore, at least one of
j_{0}
and
s_{0}\geq 2
.

(b)

s_{0}a=(s_{0}j_{0}+u)p+1
, and therefore,
s_{0}a\equiv 1~(\text{mod}~p)
. This gives
s_{0}a^{2}=a
in
\mathbb{Z}_{d}=\mathbb{Z}_{pa}
.

(c)

Let
f=a-\frac{s_{0}a-1}{p}=\frac{(p-s_{0})a+1}{p}
. Then
\frac{a+1}{p}\leq f\leq\frac{(p-1)a+1}{p}=\frac{pa-(a-1)}{p}
. So

\frac{p+2}{p}\leq f\leq a-1
. As
f
is an integer, we have
2\leq f\leq a-1
. Next,
fp=(p-s_{0})a+1\equiv 1~(\text{mod}~a)
. This gives

fp^{2}=p
in
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.

(d)

Let
g_{1}=\frac{s_{0}a+p-1}{p}s_{0}~(\text{mod}~p)
. Set
g=p-1
if
g_{1}=0
and
g_{1}-1
, otherwise. Then
0\leq g\leq p-1
. So
2\leq ga+f\leq(p-1)a+f\leq pa-1
.

(e)

Also
\text{HCF}(s_{0}a+p,pa)=1
, we can check that
(s_{0}a+p)(ga+f)\equiv 1~(\text{mod}~pa)
so that
ga+f=(s_{0}a+p)^{\wedge}
.
Indeed,
ga=\left(\frac{s_{0}a+p-1}{p}s_{0}a-a\right)

(\text{mod}~pa)
, and therefore, modulo
pa
,

ga+f=\frac{s_{0}a+p-1}{p}s_{0}a-\frac{s_{0}a-1}{p}=\frac{s_{0}a+p-1}{p}(s_{0}a-1)+1
. Using (b)
(ga+f)(s_{0}a+p)=\frac{s_{0}a+p-1}{p}(-p)+s_{0}a+p=1
.

(f)

To give an idea, for
p=2,r_{0}=s_{0}=1,s_{0}a+p=a+2,f=\frac{a+1}{2},ga+f=\frac{a+1}{2}
if it is odd and
a+\frac{a+1}{2}
, otherwise.

3.6.
Ideal number
d^{2}
for
d=p^{2}
and
d=pa
with
p
and
a
primes by optimal constraint technique for the shift and multiply, cyclic group case as in §2.3 and §3.5

We freely use the preparation in §3.5 above to apply the results in §3.1 to §3.4 effectively.

3.6.1.

We begin with the case
d=p^{2}
with
p
any prime.

Theorem 3.6.1
.

(Minimal
{\bf W}
-MASS and constraints) Let
d=p^{2}
,
p
any prime.

(i)

A convenient
{\bf W}
-MASS,
\mathcal{M}_{{\bf W}}
, is given by

\{\lambda_{(ap+r,1)},0\leq a
,
r\leq p-1\}

\bigcup\{\lambda_{(1,bp)}:0\leq b\leq p-1\}
.
Its cardinality is
p^{2}+p=p(p+1)
.

(ii)

For
0\leq r\leq p-1
,

\displaystyle\lambda(rp,p)\subset\lambda_{(ap+r,1)}\quad\text{for}\ 0\leq a\leq p-1.

Also
\lambda_{(p,0)}\subset\lambda_{(1,bp)}
for
0\leq b\leq p-1
.

Further, there are no more overlaps.

(iii)

Each overlap in (ii) above gives rise to a uniform constraint of size
p
.

Proof.

(i) and (ii) follow from easy computations in view of basic modular arithmetic.

For (iii), we divide the proof in a few parts below.

(a)

Consider
0\leq r\leq p-1
and the corresponding unitary operator
U_{(rp,p)}
.
For
0\leq k\leq d-1=p^{2}-1
,

\displaystyle U_{(rp,p)}|k\rangle=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi irk}{p}\right)|p+k\rangle.

(3.20)

So, for
0\leq t\leq p-1
,
0\leq v\leq p-1

\displaystyle U_{(rp,p)}|vp+t\rangle=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi irt}{p}\right)|(v+1)p+t\rangle.

(3.21)

Consider
0\leq u\leq p-1
and put
\beta=\exp\left(\dfrac{2\pi iu}{p}\right)
.
Set
\xi_{u,t}=\sum\limits_{v=0}^{p-1}\bar{\beta}^{v}|vp+t\rangle
.
Then

\displaystyle U_{(rp,p)}\xi_{u,t}=\exp\left(\dfrac{2\pi i(rt+u)}{p}\right)\xi_{u,t}.

(3.22)

For
0\leq c\leq p-1
, let

\displaystyle S_{r,c}=\{(u,t):rt+u=c\,(\textrm{mod}\,\,p)\}.

(3.23)

Then
\#S_{r,c}=p
and, as
c
varies,
S_{r,c}
’s are mutually disjoint with union equal to

\{(u,t):0\leq u
,
t\leq p-1\}
. Further,
U_{(rp,p)}
has

e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ic}{p}\right)}
as an eigenvalue
of multiplicity
p
with eigenspace
E_{r,c}
, the span
of
\xi_{u,t}
as
(u,t)
varies in
S_{r,c}
. We set
Q_{r,c}=

the orthogonal projection on
E_{r,c}
.
We note that
Q_{r,c}
has rank
p
.

(b)

Let
0\leq t\leq p-1
. Then for
0\leq v\leq p-1
,

U_{(p,0)}|vp+t\rangle=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi it}{p}\right)}|vp+t\rangle
.
So
e^{\left(\frac{2\pi it}{p}\right)}
is an eigenvalue of
multiplicity
p
for
U_{(p,0)}
with eigenspace

E_{t}
as the span of
\{|vp+t\rangle:0\leq v\leq p-1\}
.
Set
Q_{t}
, the orthogonal projection on
E_{t}
.
We note that
Q_{t}
has rank
p
.

(c)

Let
0\leq r\leq p-1
. Set
\mathbf{Q}^{(r)}=\{Q_{r,c}:0\leq c\leq p-1\}

and for
0\leq a\leq p-1
, let
\mathcal{P}^{(r)}_{a}

be a common orthonormal eigenvector system for
\lambda_{(ap+r,1)}
.
Then each
\mathcal{P}_{a}^{(r)}
is a
\mathbf{Q}^{(r)}
-constrained
elementary measurement,
0\leq a\leq p-1
.

(d)

Let
\mathbf{Q}^{(p)}=\{Q_{t}:0\leq t\leq p-1\}

and for
0\leq b\leq p-1
,
\mathcal{P}_{b}^{(p)}
a common orthonormal eigenvector system
for
\lambda_{(1,bp)}
.
Then
\mathcal{P}_{b}^{(p)}
is a
\mathbf{Q}^{(p)}
-constrained elementary measurement for
0\leq b\leq p-1
.

∎

Theorem 3.6.2
.

The size of pure measurement obtained by our Optimal Constraint Technique for the case
d=p^{2}
with
p
prime is the ideal number
i.e., the minimum possible ,
d^{2}=p^{4}
.

Proof.

Theorem
3.6.1
above facilitates the use of Theorem
3.1.4
or, for that matter, repeated use of Remark
3.1.2

(iii) after some relabelling. So the size of the reduced set of pure measurements is
(p+1)p\cdot p^{2}-(p+1)(p-1)p-p=p^{4}=d^{2}
.

We now proceed to obtain one such system explicitly with the help of Theorem
3.6.1
and its proof above in a few steps.

(i)

We begin with part (iii)(a) and (c) of the proof of Theorem
3.6.1
.

(a)

Fix
r
and
c
. For
0\leq t\leq p-1
, let
\xi_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\xi_{(c-rt){~(\text{mod}~p),t}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\xi_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{-\left(\frac{2\pi iv}{p}(c-rt)~(\text{mod}~p)\right)}|vp+t\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{-\left(\frac{2\pi iv}{p}(c-rt)\right)}|vp+t\rangle.

(3.24)

Then
\xi_{t}
’s span
E_{r,c}
.

(b)

Fix
0\leq a\leq p-1
temporarily. For
0\leq t\leq p-1
,

\displaystyle U_{ap+r,1}\xi_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{-\left(\frac{2\pi iv}{p}(c-rt)\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)(vp+t)\right)}|vp+t+1\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{-\left(\frac{2\pi iv(c-rt-r)}{p}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i(ap+r)t}{p^{2}}\right)}|vp+(t+1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)t\right)}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{-\left(\frac{2\pi iv(c-r(t+1))}{p}\right)}|vp+(t+1)\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i(ap+r)t}{p^{2}}\right)}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\xi_{t+1}~~~~~~~~~\text{if}~0\leq t\leq p-2\\
e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ic}{p}\right)}\xi_{0}~\text{if}~t=p-1\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\text{Consequently},~U_{ap+r,1}E_{r,c}=E_{r,c}.

(3.27)

(c)

For a
p
-tuple
{\bf b}=(b_{t})_{t=0}^{p-1}
in
\mathbb{C}^{p}
,
\eta_{{\bf b}}=\sum_{t=0}^{p-1}b_{t}\xi_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~U_{ap+r,1}\eta_{{\bf b}}=\sum_{t=0}^{p-2}b_{t}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i(ap+r)t}{p^{2}}\right)}\xi_{t+1}+b_{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i(ap+r)(p-1)}{p^{2}}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ic}{p}\right)}\xi_{0}.

(3.28)

Now by (b) above,
U_{ap+r,1}E_{r,c}=E_{r,c}
and, therefore,

\displaystyle\left(U_{ap+r,1}/E_{r,c}\right)^{-1}=\left(U_{ap+r,1}\right)^{-1}/E_{r,c}.

(3.29)

So

\displaystyle\left(U_{ap+r,1}/E_{r,c}\right)^{\ast}=\left(U_{ap+r,1}\right)^{\ast}/E_{r,c}.

(3.30)

So
U_{ap+r,1}/E_{r,c}
has
p
eigenvalues with eigenvectors in
E_{r,c}
. Let
\gamma
be
any such eigenvalue. Then by Proposition
3.5.1
(i),
e^{-\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}\frac{p(p-1)}{2}(ap+r)\right)}\gamma^{p}
is an eigenvalue of
U_{p(ap+r),p}=U_{rp,p}

restricted to
E_{r,c}
and, therefore,

\displaystyle\gamma^{p}=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}\frac{p(p-1)}{2}(ap+r)\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ic}{p}\right)}.

(3.31)

Let
\gamma
be any such eigenvalue with
\eta_{{\bf b}}
as an eigenvector. Then

\displaystyle\gamma b_{t+1}=b_{t}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)t\right)}~\text{for}~0\leq t\leq p-2

(3.32)

and

\displaystyle\gamma b_{0}=b_{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)(p-1)\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ic}{p}\right)}.

(3.33)

The converse is also true. In particular,
\eta_{{\bf b}}\neq 0
if and only if
{\bf b}\neq 0
if and only if
b_{t}\neq 0
for
0\leq t\leq p-1
.

Now the system of equations becomes

\displaystyle b_{t}

\displaystyle=\overline{\gamma}^{t}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)\frac{(t-1)t}{2}\right)}b_{0}~\text{for}~1\leq t\leq p-1,

\displaystyle\gamma b_{0}

\displaystyle=b_{p-1}.

(3.34)

Considering
t=p-1
,

\displaystyle b_{p-1}=\overline{\gamma}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)\frac{(p-1)(p-2)}{2}\right)}b_{0}.

(3.35)

So

\displaystyle b_{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)(p-1)\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ic}{p}\right)}

\displaystyle=\overline{\gamma}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)\frac{p(p-1)}{2}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ic}{p}\right)}b_{0}.

\displaystyle=\overline{\gamma}^{p-1}\gamma^{p}b_{0}

\displaystyle=\gamma b_{0}

(3.36)

Thus the last equation is automatically satisfied.

Now

\displaystyle\gamma=\gamma_{s}=e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)(p-1)\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ic}{p^{2}}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi is}{p}\right)}

\displaystyle~\text{for some}~s~\text{with}~0\leq s\leq p-1.

(3.37)

So

\displaystyle\overline{\gamma}_{s}e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)(t-1)\right)}=e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)(t-p)\right)}e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi ic}{p^{2}}\right)}e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi is}{p}\right)}

(3.38)

Hence for a unit eigenvector
\eta_{{\bf b}_{s}}
for
\gamma_{s}
, we may take

\displaystyle{\bf b}_{s}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\left(\left(e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)(t-p)\right)}e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi ic}{p^{2}}\right)}e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi is}{p}\right)}\right)^{t}\right)_{t=0}^{p-1}

(3.39)

(d)

We consolidate the conclusions in (a), (b), (c) above in a notation that can be used for the whole system later.
Let
0\leq r,a\leq p-1
. For
0\leq c,t\leq p-1
, we write

\xi(r,c,t)
for
\xi_{t}
as in (a) above, i.e.,

\displaystyle\xi(r,c,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{-\left(\frac{2\pi iv(c-rt)}{p}\right)}|vp+t\rangle.

(3.40)

We note that for
0\leq r^{\prime},c^{\prime},t^{\prime}\leq p-1
,

\displaystyle\langle\xi(r,c,t)\xi(r^{\prime},c^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle=0~

(3.41)

for
t^{\prime}\neq t
and also for
r=r^{\prime}
but
c\neq c^{\prime}
.

Next, for
0\leq c,s\leq p-1
, we write
\gamma(r,a;c,s)
for
\gamma_{s}
as in (c)
above, i.e.,

\displaystyle\gamma(r,a;c,s)=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)(p-1)\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ic}{p^{2}}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi is}{p}\right)},

(3.42)

\beta(r,a;c,s)=(\beta(r,a;c,s,t)_{t=0}^{p-1}
with

\displaystyle\beta(r,a;c,s,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{p^{2}}(ap+r)((t-p)t)\right)}e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi ict}{p^{2}}\right)}e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi ist}{p}\right)}

(3.43)

for
0\leq t\leq p-1
; and , finally

\eta(r,a;c,s)
for
\eta_{{\bf b}_{s}}
as in (c) above i.e.,

\displaystyle\eta(r,a;c,s)=\sum_{t=0}^{p-1}\beta(r,a;c,s,t)\xi(r;c,t).

(3.44)

We note that
\mathcal{E}_{a}^{(r)}=\{\eta(r,a;c,s):0\leq c,s\leq p-1\}
is a complete system of orthonormal unit eigenvectors corresponding to eigenvalues

\{\gamma(r,a;c,s):0\leq c,s\leq p-1\}
for

U_{ap+r,1}
.

This gives us a family

\{\mathcal{P}_{a}^{(r)}=\{P(r,a:c,s)=|\eta(r,a;c,s)\rangle\langle\eta(r,a;c,s)|:0\leq c,s,\leq p-1\}:0\leq r,a\leq p-1\}

of elementary
measurements
\mathcal{P}_{a}^{(r)}
’s constrained by
{\bf Q}^{(r)}
for
0\leq r,a\leq p-1
.

(ii)

We now utilize (ii)(b) and (iii)(d) of the proof of Theorem
3.6.1
above.

(a)

U_{1,0}
has a complete set
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(p)}
of orthonormal eigenvectors
\{|j\rangle:0\leq j\leq p^{2}-1\}

with corresponding eigenvalues
\{e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ij}{p^{2}}\right)}:0\leq j\leq p^{2}-1\}
. We may rewrite the parameter set
\{j:0\leq j\leq p^{2}-1\}

as
\{vp+t:0\leq v,t\leq p-1\}
as well.

(b)

Let
0\leq t\leq p-1
. Then for
0\leq v\leq p-1
,

\displaystyle U_{1,p}|vp+t\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(vp+t)\right)}|vp+t+p\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi iv}{p}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi it}{p^{2}}\right)}|(v+1)p+t\rangle

(3.45)

So as argued in (i)(c) above,
U_{1,p}/E_{t}
has
p
eigenvalues in
E_{t}
and further, for any such eigenvalue
\gamma
,

e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}\frac{p(p-1)}{2}p\right)}\gamma^{p}
is an eigenvalue of
U_{p(1,p)}=U_{(p,0)}

restricted to
E_{t}
.
Therefore,
e^{\left(-\pi i(p-1)\right)}\gamma^{p}=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi it}{p}\right)}
. Consequently
\gamma^{p}=\mp e^{\left(\frac{2\pi it}{p}\right)}
according as
p=2
or
p>2
.

Now for
p=2
, for
t=0,\gamma=\mp i
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|0\rangle\mp i|2\rangle)

work as unit eigenvectors, whereas for
t=1
,
\gamma=\pm 1
and

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|1\rangle\pm i|3\rangle)
work as eigenvectors.

For
p>2
,
\gamma=\gamma_{t,s}=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi it}{p^{2}}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi is}{p}\right)}
for some
s
with
0\leq s\leq p-1
. Simple
computations
give that for
0\leq s\leq p-1
,

\displaystyle\eta_{1;t,s}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{\pi iv(v-1)}{p}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi isv}{p}\right)}|vp+t\rangle

(3.46)

works as a unit eigenvector corresponding to the eigenvalue
\gamma_{t,s}
for

U_{1,p}/E_{t}
.

(c)

Let
p>2
and
2\leq b\leq p-1
. Let
0\leq t\leq p-1
. Since
p
is prime ,

\{kb~\text{mod}~p):1\leq k\leq p-1\}
=
\{v:1\leq v\leq p-1\}
. So

\{v+pt:0\leq v\leq p-1\}=\{kb~(\text{mod}~p)p+t:0\leq k\leq p-1\}
=

\{(kbp+t)~(\text{mod}~p^{2}:0\leq k\leq p-1\}
).
Therefore, we may say that
E_{t}
, the span of
\{|vp+t\rangle:0\leq v\leq p-1\}
, is same as the
span of
\{|kbp+t\rangle:0\leq k\leq p-1\}
in the context of
\mathbb{C}^{p^{2}}
.

Now

\displaystyle U_{1,bp}|kbp+t\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(kbp+t)\right)}|kbp+t+bp\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi it}{p^{2}}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ikb}{p}\right)}|(k+1)bp+t\rangle

(3.47)

for
0\leq k\leq p-1
.
So

\displaystyle\eta_{b;t,s}=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p^{2}}(\frac{k(k-1)}{2}bp)\right)}e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi iks}{p}\right)}|kbp+t\rangle

(3.48)

is a unit eigenvector corresponding to the eigenvalue

\gamma_{t,s}=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi it}{p^{2}}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi is}{p}\right)}

of
U_{1,bp}/E_{t}
.

(d)

We note that
\eta_{1;t,s}
in (3.33) in (b) above and the expression on RHS of
\eta_{b;t,s}
in (3.35) in (c) above for
b=1
are the same.
So we may say that for
p>2
,
1\leq b\leq p-1
,

\{\eta_{b;t,s}:0\leq t,s\leq p-1\}
is a complete system of unit eigenvectors of
U_{1,bp}
.

(e)

We set
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(p)}=\{P_{j}=|j\rangle\langle j|:~0\leq j\leq p^{2}-1\}
and for

1\leq b\leq p-1
,
\mathcal{P}_{b}^{(p)}=\{P_{b,t,s}=|\eta_{b;t,s}\rangle\langle\eta_{b;t,s}|:~0\leq t,s\leq p-1\}
and obtain elementary measurements, constrained by
{\bf Q}^{(p)}
. For notational convenience, we can even write
|j\rangle
as
\eta_{0;t,s}
for
j=sp+t,~0\leq t,s\leq p-1
.

(iii)

There are various ways in view of Theorem
3.6.1
above to obtain informationally complete families of rank one projections of the ideal size
d^{2}=p^{4}
from

\{\mathcal{P}_{b}^{(p)}:0\leq b\leq p-1\}\cup\{\mathcal{P}_{a}^{(r)}:0\leq r,a\leq p-1\}
. We just display one of them, say
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, below.

For
1\leq b\leq p-1
let
\{\mathcal{P}_{b}^{(p)\prime}=\{P_{b,t,s}:0\leq t\leq p-1,1\leq s\leq p-1\}
.

For
0\leq r\leq p-1
let
\{\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(r)\prime}=\{P(r,0;c,s):0\leq c,s\leq p-1,(c,s)\neq(0,0)\}
and let for
1\leq a\leq p-1
,
\mathcal{P}_{a}^{(r)\prime}=\{P(r,a;c,s):0\leq c\leq p-1,1\leq s\leq p-1\}
. Now set

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(p)}\cup\cup_{b=1}^{p-1}\mathcal{P}_{b}^{(p)\prime}\cup\cup_{0\leq r,a\leq p-1}\mathcal{P}_{a}^{(r)\prime}.

We verify that size of

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is
p^{2}+(p-1)(p^{2}-p)+(p(p^{2}-1)+p(p-1)(p^{2}-p))=p^{4}
.

∎

We now come to quantum mechanical overlaps
\text{tr}(P_{\eta}P_{\eta^{\prime}})
, or,
for that matter
\langle\eta,\eta^{\prime}\rangle|^{2}
as indicated in Remark
3.5.5
(iv).
We first give it for
d=2^{2}=4
and then
d=p^{2}
with
p
an odd prime.

Example 3.6.3
.

Let
d=4=2^{2}
. Then from the proof of Theorem
3.6.2
, parts (ii) and (i)(d) give the following which can be computed directly as well.

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\{|j\rangle:j=0,1,2,3\}~\text{coming from}~U_{1,0},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|0\rangle-i|2\rangle),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|0\rangle+i|2\rangle),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|1\rangle+i|3\rangle),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|1\rangle-i|3\rangle)\}

\displaystyle~\text{coming from}~U_{1,2},

(3.49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\{\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle+|2\rangle)+(|1\rangle+|3\rangle)),\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle+|2\rangle)-(|1\rangle+|3\rangle)),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle-|2\rangle)-i(|1\rangle-|3\rangle)),\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle-|2\rangle)+i(|1\rangle-|3\rangle))\}

\displaystyle~\text{coming from}~U_{0,1},

(3.50)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\{\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle+|2\rangle)-i(|1\rangle+|3\rangle)),\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle+|2\rangle)+i(|1\rangle+|3\rangle)),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle-|2\rangle)-(|1\rangle-|3\rangle)),\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle-|2\rangle)+(|1\rangle-|3\rangle))\}

\displaystyle~\text{coming from}~U_{2,1},

(3.51)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\{\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle+|2\rangle)+\left(\frac{1-i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)(|1\rangle-|3\rangle)),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle+|2\rangle)-\left(\frac{1-i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)(|1\rangle-|3\rangle)),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle-|2\rangle)-\left(\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)(|1\rangle-|3\rangle)),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle-|2\rangle)+\left(\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)(|1\rangle-|3\rangle))\},

\displaystyle~\text{coming from}~U_{1,1},

(3.52)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\{\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle+|2\rangle)-\left(\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)(|1\rangle-|3\rangle)),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle+|2\rangle)+i\left(\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)(|1\rangle-|3\rangle)),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle-|2\rangle)-\left(\frac{1-i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)(|1\rangle+|3\rangle)),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((|0\rangle-|2\rangle)+\left(\frac{1-i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)(|1\rangle+|3\rangle))\}

\displaystyle~\text{coming from}~U_{3,1}.

(3.53)

The arrangements have been written with convenience of constraints and quantum mechanical overlaps in mind. Quantum mechanical overlaps are easily
seen to be
0,1/2,1/4,1
. This is in line with 2.4.2 above.

Example 3.6.4
.

Let
d=p^{2}
with
p
an odd prime. There is no hope of mutual unbiasedness in this case as well as already indicated in Remark
3.5.5
Step (iv).
We give quantum mechanical overlaps for the minimal system given in Theorem
3.6.2
and show that they have values
0,1,1/p,1/p^{2}
.

First we note a few immediate useful facts based on Remark
3.5.5
Step (iv) and parts (i)(d) and (ii) of proof of Theorem
3.6.2
above.
Let
0\leq r,a,c,s,r^{\prime},a^{\prime},c^{\prime},s^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}

\leq p-1
with
(r,a)\neq(r^{\prime},a^{\prime})
and
0\leq b,b^{\prime}\leq p-1
with

b\neq b^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\text{(i)}~~\langle\eta(r,a;c,s)|\eta(r^{\prime},a^{\prime};c^{\prime},s^{\prime})\rangle=\sum_{t=0}^{p-1}\overline{\beta(r,a;c,s,t)}\beta(r^{\prime},a^{\prime};c^{\prime},s^{\prime},t)\langle\xi(r,c,t)|\xi(r^{\prime},c^{\prime},t)\rangle

\displaystyle\text{simply because}~\langle\xi(r,c,t)|\xi(r^{\prime},c^{\prime},t^{\prime}\rangle=0~\text{for}~t\neq t^{\prime}.

\displaystyle\text{(ii)}~\langle\eta(r,a;c,s)|\eta(r,a^{\prime},c^{\prime},s^{\prime})\rangle=0

\displaystyle\text{for}~c\neq c^{\prime}~\text{simply because}~\langle\xi(r,c,t)|\xi(r,c^{\prime},t^{\prime}\rangle=0~\text{for}~c\neq c^{\prime}.

\displaystyle\text{(iii)}~\langle\eta_{b;t,s}|\eta_{b^{\prime};t^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\rangle=0

\displaystyle\text{for}~t\neq t^{\prime}~\text{simply because}~\langle vp+t|v^{\prime}p+t^{\prime}\rangle=0~\text{for}~0\leq v,v^{\prime}\leq p-1~\text{and}~t\neq t^{\prime}.

(iv) All other inner products like
\langle\eta|\eta^{\prime}\rangle
for
\eta\neq\eta^{\prime}
are likely to be non zero and our interest is in

\text{tr}(P_{\eta}P_{\eta^{\prime}})=|\langle\eta|\eta^{\prime}\rangle|^{2}
and we proceed to compute such numbers i.e., non trivial quantum mechanical
overlaps in the order we like.

(v)
|\langle\eta_{0;t,s}|\eta_{b^{\prime};t,s^{\prime}}\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{p}
for
b^{\prime}\neq 0
.

(vi)
|\langle\eta_{0;t,s}|\eta_{r,a;c,s^{\prime}}\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{p^{2}}
.

(vii) We next consider
r\neq r^{\prime}
.

(a)

\displaystyle\langle\xi(r,c,t)|\xi(r^{\prime},c^{\prime},t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}v(c-rt)-\frac{2\pi i}{p}v(c^{\prime}-r^{\prime}t)\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi iv}{p}((c-c^{\prime})-(r-r^{\prime})t)\right)}

(3.54)

Because
r^{\prime\prime}=r-r^{\prime}\neq 0
,
\{r^{\prime\prime}t~(\text{mod}~p):1\leq t\leq p-1\}

=\{u:1\leq u\leq p-1\}
. So there
is a unique
t
, say
t_{r^{\prime\prime}}
with
0\leq t_{r^{\prime\prime}}\leq p-1
such that
(c-c^{\prime})-r^{\prime\prime}t=0~(\text{mod}~p)
.

(b)

For
t\neq t_{r^{\prime\prime}}
,

\displaystyle\langle\xi(r,c,t)|\xi(r^{\prime},c^{\prime},t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi iv}{p}((c-c^{\prime})-(r-r^{\prime})t)\right)}=0,

(3.55)

simply because
0<(c-c^{\prime})-(r-r^{\prime})t~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1
.

(c)

For
t=t_{r^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\langle\xi(r,c,t)|\xi(r^{\prime},c^{\prime},t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{v=0}^{p-1}1=1.

(3.56)

In particular,
\xi(r,c,t_{r^{\prime\prime}})
and
\xi(r^{\prime},c^{\prime},t_{r^{\prime\prime}})
are multiples of each other by scalars of modulus one. Indeed, they both coincide with
\xi_{t_{r^{\prime\prime}}}
as in (3.13) above.

(d)

By (b) and (c) just above and (i) above

\displaystyle\langle\eta(r,a;c,s)|\eta(r^{\prime},a^{\prime};c^{\prime},s^{\prime})\rangle=\overline{\beta(r,a;c,s,t_{r^{\prime\prime}})}\beta(r^{\prime},a^{\prime};c^{\prime},s^{\prime},t_{r^{\prime\prime}})

(3.57)

and therefore

\displaystyle|\langle\eta(r,a;c,s)|\eta(r^{\prime},a^{\prime};c^{\prime},s^{\prime})\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{p^{2}}

(3.58)

(viii) For
a\neq a^{\prime}
, by (i) above

\displaystyle\langle\eta(r,a;c,s)|\eta(r,a^{\prime};c,s^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{p-1}\overline{\beta(r,a;c,s,t)}\beta(r,a^{\prime};c,s^{\prime},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(a-a^{\prime})\frac{(p-t)t}{2}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(s-s^{\prime})t\right)}.

(3.59)

Without loss of generality, we may take
a>a^{\prime}
. Because
p
is odd,
(p-t)t
is even for
0\leq t\leq p-1
. So we may replace
a-a^{\prime}

by
a-a^{\prime}+p
, if the need be, in the above expression. Also
a-a^{\prime}
or
a-a^{\prime}+p
is even, say
2v
with
1\leq v\leq p-1
. Thus the
expression on the RHS becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}v(p-t)t\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(s-s^{\prime})t\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{-2\pi i}{p}vt^{2}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(s-s^{\prime})t\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{t=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}((p-v)t^{2}+(s-s^{\prime})t\right)}

(3.60)

Now

\displaystyle|\langle\eta(r,a;c,s)|\eta(r,a^{\prime};c,s^{\prime})\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\sum_{t,t^{\prime}=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}v(t^{\prime 2}-t^{2})\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(s-s^{\prime})(t-t^{\prime})\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\sum_{t,t^{\prime}=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}v(t^{\prime}+t)\right)(t^{\prime}-t)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(s^{\prime}-s)(t^{\prime}-t)\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0\leq t^{\prime}+t~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1}}{{0\leq t^{\prime}-t~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1}}}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}v(t^{\prime}+t)\right)(t^{\prime}-t)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(s^{\prime}-s)(t^{\prime}-t)\right)}

(3.61)

Because
1\leq v~\leq p-1
,
there is a unique

u
with
0\leq u~\leq p-1
and
uv+(s^{\prime}-s)=0~(\text{mod}~p)
. So, the expression in (3.48)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\Big[\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0\leq t^{\prime}-t~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1}}{{t^{\prime}+t=u~(\text{mod}~p)}}}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(v(t^{\prime}+t)+(s^{\prime}-s))(t^{\prime}-t)\right)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0\leq t^{\prime}-t~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1}}{{t^{\prime}+t\neq u~(\text{mod}~p)}}}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(v(t^{\prime}+t)+(s^{\prime}-s))(t^{\prime}-t)\right)}\Big]

(3.62)

Now, for
t^{\prime}+t\neq u~(\text{mod}~p)
,
e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(v(t+t^{\prime})+(s^{\prime}-s))\right)}
is a
p^{\text{th}}
root of unity other than
1
, so

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq t^{\prime}-t~(\text{mod}~p)~\leq p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(v(t^{\prime}+t)+(s^{\prime}-s))(t^{\prime}-t)\right)}=0.

(3.63)

And for
t^{\prime}+t=u~(\text{mod}~p)
,
e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(v(t^{\prime}+t)+(s^{\prime}-s))(t^{\prime}-t)\right)}
is 1 for

0\leq t^{\prime}-t~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1
. So the first sum is
p
. Therefore
|\langle\eta(r,a^{\prime};c,s)|\eta(r,a^{\prime};c,s^{\prime})\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{p}
.

(ix) We now come to the case
0\neq b\neq b^{\prime}\neq 0
.

(a) We first note that because
p
is odd, either
b
or
b+p
is even and, therefore, has the form
2v
with
0<v\leq p-1
. Now for

0\leq k\leq p-1
,
|kbp+t\rangle=|k(b+p)p+t\rangle
and

\exp\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(\frac{k(k-1)}{2}b)\right)=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(\frac{k(k-1)}{2}(b+p)\right)
as well. So we may
replace
b
by
2v
in the expression for
\eta(b;t,s)
in (ii)(c) of the proof of Theorem
3.6.2
above by

\displaystyle\eta(2v;t,s)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(k-1)kv\right)}e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{p}ks\right)}|2kvp+t\rangle

(3.64)

Similar arguments apply to
b^{\prime}
with
v
replaced by
v^{\prime}
satisfying
0<v^{\prime}\leq p-1
and
2v^{\prime}=b^{\prime}
or
b^{\prime}+p
according
as
b^{\prime}
is even or odd.

(b) Because
1\leq v\neq v^{\prime}\leq p-1
,
there is a unique
k_{0}
with
1\leq k_{0}\leq p-1
satisfying
v=k_{0}v^{\prime}~(\text{mod}~p)
. Then for
0\leq k\leq p-1
,
kv=kk_{0}v^{\prime}~(\text{mod}~p)
and, therefore,
|2kvp+t\rangle=|2kk_{0}v^{\prime}p+t\rangle
. So

\displaystyle\langle\eta(b;t,s)|\eta(b^{\prime};t,s^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\eta(2v;t,s)|\eta(2v^{\prime};t,s^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(-(k-1)kv+(kk_{0}-1)kk_{0}v^{\prime}+ks-kk_{0}s^{\prime})\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}((k^{2}(k_{0}-1)v+k(s-k_{0}s^{\prime})\right)}

(3.65)

The sum has the same form as that in (viii) above (with
p-v
replaced by
(k_{0}-1)v
and
s-s^{\prime}
by
s-k_{0}s^{\prime}
). So

|\langle\eta(b;t,s)|\eta(b^{\prime},t,s^{\prime}\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{p}
.

(x) Finally, for
b\neq 0
, we consider the quantum mechanical overlap
\text{tr}(\mathcal{P}(r,a;c,s)

P(b;t,s^{\prime}))
.

(a) We first note that for
t^{\prime}\neq t
,
\langle\xi(r;c,t^{\prime})|\eta(b;t,s^{\prime})\rangle=0
.

(b)

\displaystyle\langle\xi(r;c,t)|\eta(b;t,s^{\prime})\rangle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}kb(c-rt)\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}\frac{k(k-1)}{2}b\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}ks^{\prime}\right)}

(3.66)

We argue as in (ix) above and replace
b
by
2v
with
v=\frac{1}{2}b
if
b
is even and
\frac{1}{2}(b+p)
, if
b
is odd. Then the above expression becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}2kv(c-rt)\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}k(k-1)v\right)}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}ks^{\prime}\right)}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(k(\frac{2\pi i}{p}(2v(c-rt)+(k-1)v+s^{\prime})\right)}

(3.67)

Because
0<v\leq p-1
, there is a unique
u
with
0\leq u\leq p-1
and
uv=s^{\prime}~(\text{mod}~p)
. So the above expression, viz.

\langle\xi(r;c,t)|\eta(b;t,s^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}e^{\left(k(\frac{2\pi i}{p}v(2(c-rt)+(k-1)+u)\right)}

(3.68)

So

\displaystyle|\langle\xi(r;c,t)|\eta(b;t,s^{\prime})\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\sum_{0\leq k,k_{1}\leq p-1}e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{p}v(k-k_{1})(2(c-rt)+(k+k_{1})-1+u)\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\Big[\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0\leq k-k_{1}~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1}}{{k+k_{1}+2(c-rt)-1+u=0~(\text{mod}~p)}}}~1

\displaystyle+\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0\leq k-k_{1}~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1}}{{0\leq k+k_{1}~(\text{mod}~p)}}{\neq-2(c-rt)+1-u~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1}}e^{\left((k-k_{1})(\text{mod}~p)(\frac{2\pi i}{p}v(2(c-rt)+(k+k_{1})-1+u)\right)}\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}

(3.69)

simply because
0<v(2(c-rt)+(k+k_{1})-1+u)~(\text{mod}~p)\leq p-1
in each term of the second summations and, therefore, arguments like those in (viii) above
apply.

We may combine the above two examples into a single theorem below, if we like.

Theorem 3.6.5
.

The quantum mechanical overlaps
\text{tr}(\mathcal{P}_{\eta}\mathcal{P}_{\eta^{\prime}})
arising from
\mathcal{P}
as in
part (iii) of Theorem
3.6.2
above for
d=p^{2}
,
p
any prime number have values
0,{1}/{p},{1}/{p^{2}},1
.

3.6.2.
Case d=6

We now come to the simplest case of a non-square composite number of Example 2.3.

Example 3.6.6

{\bf W}

taken away from all). To be specific, the following Table in Figure 6 gives a good idea of the situation where we have written only convenient

We now develop the optimal simultaneous constraint technique in different steps.

(i) We start with the simplest unitary
U_{(1,0)}
given by
U_{(1,0)}|k\rangle=e^{\frac{\pi i}{3}k}|k\rangle
for
0\leq k\leq 5
.
It generates
{\bf W}
-MASS
{\bf V}_{1}
.

(a)

U_{(1,0)}
has unit eigenvectors
\{\xi_{1,k}=|k\rangle,0\leq k\leq 5\}
with corresponding eigenvalues
\{e^{\frac{\pi i}{3}k},0\leq k\leq 5\}

i.e.,
\{1,-\omega^{2},\omega,-1,\omega^{2},-\omega\}
.

(b)

U_{(2,0)}=U_{(1,0)}^{2}
has eigenvalues
1,\omega=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}},\omega^{2}=e^{\frac{4\pi i}{3}}
each of multiplicity 2, with
the corresponding eigenspaces the span
E_{1,0}
of
\xi_{1,0}=|0\rangle
and
\xi_{1,3}=|3\rangle
, the span
E_{1,1}
of
\xi_{1,1}=|1\rangle

and
\xi_{1,4}=|4\rangle
and the span
E_{1,2}
of
\xi_{1,2}=|2\rangle
and
\xi_{1,5}=|5\rangle
respectively

(c)

U_{(3,0)}=U_{(1,0)}^{3}
has eigenvalues
1
and
-1
each of multiplicity 3, with
the corresponding eigenspaces the span
G_{1,0}
of
\xi_{1,0}=|0\rangle
,
\xi_{1,2}=|2\rangle
and
\xi_{1,4}=|4\rangle
, and
the span
G_{1,1}
of
\xi_{1,1}=|1\rangle
,
\xi_{1,3}=|3\rangle
and
\xi_{1,5}=|5\rangle
respectively.

(d)

Setting
P_{1,k}=|\xi_{1,k}\rangle\langle\xi_{1,k}|=|k\rangle\langle k|
for
0\leq k\leq 5
and
Q_{1,0},Q_{1,1},Q_{1,2}
projections
on
E_{1,0},E_{1,1},E_{1,2}
we have
Q_{1,0}=P_{1,0}+P_{1,3},~Q_{1,1}=P_{1,1}+P_{1,4}
and
Q_{1,2}=P_{1,2}+P_{1,5}
.

(e)

For the projections
R_{1,0}
and
R_{1,1}
on
G_{1,0}
and
G_{1,1}
respectively, we have
R_{1,0}=P_{1,0}+P_{1,2}+P_{1,4}

and
R_{1,1}=P_{1,1}+P_{1,3}+P_{1,5}
.

(f)

,

(ii) We now come to the unitary
U_{(0,1)}

{\bf V}_{2}

.

to (h) Items (b) to (h) in (i) above now have obvious analogues with

,

and

for

(iii) We consider the unitary
U_{(1,1)}
given by
U_{(1,1)}|k\rangle=e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{3}k\right)}|k+1\rangle
for

{\bf W}
-MASS

{\bf V}_{3}
.

(a)

Simple computations give that
U_{(1,1)}
has unit eigenvectors

\{\xi_{3,k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\sum_{s=0}^{5}e^{\left(-\frac{(2k+1)s\pi i}{6}\right)}e^{\left(\frac{\pi is(s-1)}{6}\right)}|s\rangle:0\leq k\leq 5\}

with corresponding eigenvalues

.

(b)

By Proposition
3.5.1
(or direct computation)
U_{(2,2)}=e^{\left(-\frac{\pi i}{3}\right)}U_{(1,1)}^{2}
. So
U_{(2,2)}
has eigenvalues

1,\omega=e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)},\omega^{2}=e^{\left(\frac{4\pi i}{3}\right)}
each of multiplicity 2 with corresponding

E_{3,0}
of

\xi_{3,0}
and
\xi_{3,3}
, the span
E_{3,1}
of
\xi_{3,1}
and
\xi_{3,4}
, and the span of
E_{3,2}
of

and

\xi_{3,5}
respectively.

(c)

3.5.1
(or, direct computations ),

. So

has eigenvalues
-i
and
i
each of multiplicity

3
with corresponding eigenspaces the span

of

,

and
\xi_{3,4}
, and the span

of

\xi_{3,1}
,
\xi_{3,3}
and
\xi_{3,5}
respectively.

(d)

to (g) Items (d) to (g) in (i) above now have obvious analogues with
\xi_{1,k}
,
P_{1,k}
,
E_{1,r}
,
Q_{1,r}
,

and
R_{1,r^{\prime}}
replaced by
\xi_{3,k}
,
P_{3,k}
,

,

,

and
R_{3,r^{\prime}}
respectively for
0\leq k\leq 5,~0\leq r\leq 2,~0\leq r^{\prime}\leq 1
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
{\bf Q}_{1}
and
{\bf R}_{1}
replaced by

\mathcal{P}_{3}
,
{\bf Q}_{3}
and
{\bf R}_{3}
respectively.

(iv) Now one constraint determined by
\lambda_{(4,2)}=\lambda_{(2,4)}
remains to be explicitly expressed. The easiest way is
to consider the unitary
U_{(1,2)}
given by
U_{(1,2)}|k\rangle=e^{\left(\frac{\pi ik}{3}\right)}|k+2\rangle
for
0\leq k\leq 5
.
It generates the
{\bf W}
-MASS
{\bf V}_{4}
.

(a)

Simple computations give that
U_{(1,2)}
has unit eigenvectors

\displaystyle\xi_{4,0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|0\rangle+|2\rangle+e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}|4\rangle)

\displaystyle\xi_{4,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|0\rangle+e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}|2\rangle+e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}|4\rangle)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|0\rangle+e^{\left(\frac{4pii}{3}\right)}|2\rangle+e^{\left(\frac{4\pi i}{3}\right)}|4\rangle),

\displaystyle\xi_{4,4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|0\rangle+e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}|2\rangle+|4\rangle)

(3.70)

with corresponding eigenvalues as
1,e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)},e^{\left(\frac{4\pi i}{3}\right)}
respectively, on the one hand,
and

\displaystyle\xi_{4,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|1\rangle+|3\rangle+e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}|5\rangle)

\displaystyle\xi_{4,3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|1\rangle+e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}|3\rangle+e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}|5\rangle)

\displaystyle\xi_{4,5}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|0\rangle+e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}|2\rangle+|5\rangle)

(3.71)

with corresponding eigenvalues

e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{3}\right)},e^{\pi i}=-1,e^{\left(\frac{5\pi i}{3}\right)}
respectively, on the otherhand.

(b)

By Proposition
3.5.1
(or, direct computations ),
U_{(2,4)}=e^{\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}U_{(1,2)}^{2}
,
so
U_{(2,4)}
has eigenvalues
e^{\left(\frac{4\pi i}{3}\right)},1
and
e^{\left(\frac{2\pi i}{3}\right)}
each of multiplicity

2
with corresponding eigenspaces the span of
E_{4,0}
of
\xi_{4,0}
and
\xi_{4,3}
, the span
E_{4,1}
of

\xi_{4,1}
and
\xi_{4,4}
, and the span
E_{4,2}
of
\xi_{4,2}
and
\xi_{4,5}
respectively.

(c)

By Proposition
3.5.1
(or, direct computations ),
U_{(3,0)}=U_{(1,2)}^{3}
.
So
U_{(3,0)}
has eigenvalues
1
and
-1
each of multiplicity

3
, with eigenspaces the span
G_{4,0}
of
\xi_{4,0},\xi_{4,2}
and
\xi_{4,4}
, and the span
G_{4,1}
of

\xi_{4,1},\xi_{4,3}
and
\xi_{4,5}
respectively. This is in line with (i)(c) because
G_{1,0}=G_{4,0}
and
G_{1,1}=G_{4,1}
.

(d)

to (h) Items (d) to (h) in (i) above now have obvious analogues with changes as indicated in (iii) (d) to (h) above.

(i)

The choice of
U_{1,2}
that generates
{\bf V}_{4}
is easier than that of generators coming from any other in the fourth row.

(j)

We have an interesting alternative provided by the technique of determining
U_{(m,n)}
from
U_{(pm,pn)}
for
p
, a prime as
indicated in Remark
3.5.5
or the proof of Theorem
3.6.2
. We will use the information in (i)(c) about
U_{(3,0)}
, which is equal to

U_{(1,2)}^{3}
as well. We note that
U_{(1,2)}G_{1,0}=G_{1,0}
and
U_{(1,2)}G_{1,1}=G_{1,1}
. So

\left(U_{(1,2)}/G_{1,0}\right)^{\ast}=U_{(1,2)}^{\ast}/G_{1,0}
and

\left(U_{(1,2)}/G_{1,1}\right)^{\ast}=U_{(1,2)}^{\ast}/G_{1,1}
. So
U_{(1,2)}
restricted to
G_{1,0}
and to
G_{1,1}
have three
eigenvalues each with unit eigenvectors present in
G_{1,0}
and
G_{1,1}
respectively. Thus, we may solve for scalars

\alpha
,
\beta
,
\mu_{k}
’s and
\nu_{k}
’s that satisfy

\displaystyle U_{(1,2)}\left(\sum_{k=0,2,4}\mu_{k}~|k\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha\left(\sum_{k=0,2,4}\mu_{k}~|k\rangle\right)

\displaystyle U_{(1,2)}\left(\sum_{k=1,3,5}\nu_{k}~|k\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\beta\left(\sum_{k=1,3,5}\nu_{k}~|k\rangle\right)~\text{and},

\displaystyle|\mu_{0}|^{2}+|\mu_{2}|^{2}+|\mu_{4}|^{2}

\displaystyle=1=|\nu_{1}|^{2}+|\nu_{3}|^{2}+|\nu_{5}|^{2}.

(3.72)

We obtain the system as in part (a) above for
\mu_{0}\geq 0,\nu_{0}\geq 0
.

We now have all the constraints in hand, It is as if
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{P}_{3},\mathcal{P}_{4}
are working as anchors for them.

used in (i) to (iv) above for further use as well. We refer to

(a)

in the corresponding listing . We note that
(2m,2n)=(2,0)~\text{or}~(0,2)
according as
(m,n)=(4,3)~\text{or}~(3,4)
. This looks
after
{\bf V}_{8}

These are in the third column. So from (b) above, the eigenvalues of

noted in this order, and, therefore, for

for the corresponding listing. So a cyclic permutation is needed to arrive at the listing for
U_{(2,0)}
and
U_{(0,2)}
in (i) and (ii) above

for

(a)

(c)

Now the interesting point is that we need not actually compute
\mathcal{P}_{j}
in full for
j\geq 5
. It is enough to just obtain
\xi_{j,0}
and

\xi_{j,1}
and the corresponding
P_{j,0}
and
P_{j,1}
for
j\geq 5
using our simultaneous constraint technique and all the observations
made above. We prefer to state it as a theorem :

Theorem 3.6.7
.

Simultaneous constraint technique for
d=6
gives the ideal number
d^{2}=36
of informationally complete rank one projections.

Proof.

We explain the technique and prove the theorem in different steps based on Example
3.6.6
above which we freely use together with notation,
terminology and results there.

(i)

We obtain the anchors
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
,
\mathcal{P}_{3}
for
{\bf V}_{1}
,
{\bf V}_{2}
,
{\bf V}_{3}
respectively as in 3.6.6 (i)
to (iii),
these have the trivial constraint
\{I_{6}\}
. So for estimation purposes, by the Constraint Technique 3.1.2,
\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}\cup\mathcal{P}_{3}

can be replaced by
\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}=\{P_{2,k}:0\leq k\leq 4\}
and

\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\prime}=\{P_{3,k}:0\leq k\leq 4\}
( or, by deleting any
P_{2,k_{2}}
and any
P_{3,k_{3}}
with
0\leq k_{2},k_{3}\leq 5
, for that matter,
and we arbitrarily choose
k_{2}=5=k_{3}
). It is as if we deleted
\xi_{2,5}
and
\xi_{3,5}
from the complete orthonormal bases for
U_{(0,1)}

and
U_{(1,1)}
respectively obtained in Example
3.6.6
(ii) and (iii).

(ii)

We now come to
\mathcal{P}_{4}
as obtained in Example
3.6.6
(iv). Then
{\bf R}_{4}={\bf R}_{1}
together with the listing as indicated in
parts (i) and (iv) of Example
3.6.6
. So
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{4}
are
{\bf R}_{1}
–constrained. By the Constraint Technique 3.1.2,

\mathcal{P}_{4}

can be replaced by
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{\prime}=\{P_{4,0},P_{4,1},P_{4,2},P_{4,3}\}
for estimation purposes. To elaborate,
P_{4,4}=R_{1,0}-P_{4,0}-P_{4,2}

and
P_{4,5}=R_{1,1}-P_{4,1}-P_{4,3}
. ( Once again, the choice of deleting
P_{4,4}
out of
\{P_{4,0},P_{4,2},P_{4,4}\}
and of

P_{4,5}
out of
\{P_{4,1},P_{4,3},P_{4,5}\}
is arbitrary. We shall not repeat this kind of noting any more in this proof). It is as if we
deleted
\xi_{4,4}
and
\xi_{4,5}
from the complete orthonormal basis
\{\xi_{4,k}:0\leq k\leq 5\}
of
U_{(1,2)}
obtained in Example

3.6.6
(iv).

(iii)

We now have all the constraints
{\bf R}_{1},{\bf R}_{2},{\bf R}_{3},{\bf Q}_{1},{\bf Q}_{2},{\bf Q}_{3},{\bf Q}_{4}
with us explicitly
expressed in terms of
\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{P}_{4}^{\prime}
.

(iv)

We now come to
\mathcal{P}_{j}
for
j\geq 5
. We claim that for each
j\geq 5
, for estimation purposes,
\mathcal{P}_{j}
can be replaced by

\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}=\{P_{j,0},P_{j,1}\}
. That is the gist of the simultaneous constraint technique which we explain.

(a)

By Example
3.6.6
(vi)(b) there is a tuple
(0_{j},1_{j},2_{j})
of distinct numbers 0,1,2 in some order with
Q_{j,0}=Q_{u_{j},0_{j}},Q_{j,1}=Q_{u_{j},1_{j}}

and
Q_{j,2}=Q_{u_{j},2_{j}}
.

By combining this with Example
3.6.6
(vi) (a), we obtain

\displaystyle P_{j,0}+P_{j,2}+P_{j,4}=R_{j,0}=R_{v_{j},0},

\displaystyle P_{j,1}+P_{j,3}+P_{j,5}=R_{j,1}=R_{v_{j},1},

\displaystyle P_{j,0}+P_{j,3}=Q_{j,0}=Q_{u_{j},0_{j}},

\displaystyle P_{j,1}+P_{j,4}=Q_{j,1}=Q_{u_{j},1_{j}},~~~~~~~~~~\text{and}

\displaystyle P_{j,2}+P_{j,5}=Q_{j,2}=Q_{u_{j},2_{j}}.

(3.73)

(b)

We can solve for
P_{j,k},~k=2,3,4,5
in terms of
P_{j,0}
,
P_{j,1}
,
Q_{u_{j},0_{j}}
,
Q_{u_{j},1_{j}}
,
Q_{u_{j},2_{j}}

,
R_{v_{j},0}
and
R_{v_{j},1}
from (a) and obtain the following in succession

\displaystyle P_{j,3}=Q_{u_{j},0_{j}}-P_{j,0},

\displaystyle P_{j,4}=Q_{u_{j},1_{j}}-P_{j,1},

\displaystyle P_{j,2}=R_{v_{j},0}-P_{j,0}-Q_{u_{j},1_{j}}+P_{j,1},

\displaystyle P_{j,5}=Q_{u_{j},2_{j}}-R_{v_{j},0}+P_{j,0}++Q_{u_{j},1_{j}}-P_{j,1}.

(3.74)

For illustration,

,

\displaystyle P_{12,3}=Q_{4,2}-P_{12,0},

\displaystyle P_{12,4}=Q_{4,0}-P_{12,1},

\displaystyle P_{12,2}=R_{3,0}-P_{12,0}-Q_{4,0}+P_{12,1},

\displaystyle P_{12,5}=Q_{4,1}-R_{3,0}+P_{12,0}++Q_{4,0}-P_{12,1}.

(3.75)

We give a pictorial representation of information in (a) and (b) above in Figures 9 and 10.

(d)

It is as if we deleted
\xi_{j,2},~\xi_{j,3},~\xi_{j,4},~\xi_{j,5}
from the complete orthonormal system of
U_{m,n}
chosen to
generate
{\bf V}_{j}
. As a matter of fact, we need not compute them and manage with
\xi_{j,0}
and
\xi_{j,1}
, with eigenvalues
1
and

e^{\frac{\pi i}{3}}
, if
mn
is even, or,
e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}},e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}}e^{\frac{i\pi}{3}}=i
, if
mn
is odd for
the
(m,n)
chosen to generate
{\bf V}_{j}
.

(v)

There is no hope of mutual unbiasedness as seen in the case of
d=p^{2}
above in Theorem
3.6.5
. We note that

\displaystyle\text{tr}(P_{j,k}P_{1,k^{\prime}})=|\langle\xi_{j,k},\xi_{1,k^{\prime}}\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{6}~\text{for}~j=2~\text{or}~3,

(3.76)

whereas

\displaystyle\text{tr}(P_{4,k}P_{1,k^{\prime}})=|\langle\xi_{4,k},\xi_{1,k^{\prime}\rangle}|^{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{1}{3}~\text{for}~k,~k^{\prime}~\text{both odd or both even}\\
\\
0~\text{if one of}~k,k^{\prime}~\text{is odd
and the other even}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\text{tr}(P_{9,0}P_{2,0})=|\langle\xi_{9,0},\xi_{2,0}\rangle|^{2}=|\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\sum_{s=0,2,4}|s\rangle,\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\sum_{s=0}^{5}|s\rangle\rangle|=\frac{1}{2}

(3.80)

We shall elaborate more on this in Remark
3.6.8
(vi) below.

(vi)

The total number of rank one projections in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{P}_{4}^{\prime}\cup\cup_{j=5}^{12}\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}
is
6+5+5+4+8\times 2=36
. Also
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is complete in the sense
that each
U_{jk}
is a linear combination of members from
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and thus
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a basis for
M_{6}
. So the
technique gives the ideal numer 36 of such rank one projections.

∎

Remark 3.6.8
.

It is clear from the discussion particularly, Figures 6 to 10 in Example
3.6.6
and the proof of Theorem
3.6.7
that there are many choices
of choosing
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of the ideal number 36 available. We note some of them :

(i)

If we choose another generator of any
\lambda_{(m,n)}
of full size, then the system of eigenvectors remains the same and only
eigenvalues undergo a permutation and thus, the listing of new system, say,
\mathcal{S}_{j}=\{S_{j,k}:k=0,\cdots,5\}
will just
be a permutation of
\mathcal{P}_{j}=\{P_{j,k}:k=0,\cdots,5\}
for
1\leq j\leq 12
.

(ii)

The situation is similar to that of (i) above for the overlaps by replacing eigenvectors by eigenprojections. So the listing of the
new system, say,
\chi_{j}=\{\chi_{jk},k=0,1,2\}
will just be a permutation of
{\bf Q}_{j}=\{Q_{jk}:k=0,1,2\}
. We re-inforce that for
R_{j}
’s no change
is needed.

(iii)

The care taken to match multiple
(2m,2n)
by
2
of
(m,n)
made the matching more transparent and practical.

(iv)

The net result is that the rows and columns in Figures 7 and 8 remain intact, though elements may undergo permutations and the sums in the
last row or column may undergo permutations. In other words, we have cosets of
\{0,3\}
and
\{0,2,4\}
occurring in rows and columns
respectively as subscripts. So, to figure out the sums of rows and columns, it is enough to check for two different rank one projections
in different cosets. In this Remark
3.1.2
comes in handy.

(v)

Now we obtain a basis for rank one projections for
M_{6}
. We need only two suitable such projections for each
j\geq 5
.

(a) We can delete any row and any column and retain the rest. This gives us choices like
\{P_{j,0},P_{j,2}\}
,

\{P_{j,1},P_{j,3}\}
etc..

(b) Another one is to delete any row and then choose one each from the remaining rows. This gives us extra choices like
\{P_{1,0},P_{1,1}\}
made
in the proof of Theorem
3.6.7
. To go back to the starting point, we can can start with any generator
U_{(m,n)}

of the
{\bf W}
-MASS
{\bf V}_{j}
, and we can
take eigenvectors corresponding to any two eigenvalues with
different squares and then take corresponding projections.

(c) In fact, in view of analogue of (i)(h), all we need is, to figure out
u_{j}
and
v_{j}
and take any distinct
Q_{u_{j},t_{1}},Q_{u_{j},t_{2}}
and take
products
Q_{u_{j},t_{1}}R_{v_{j},0}
and
Q_{u_{j},t_{2}}R_{v_{j},0}
.

(vi)

The right choice in (v) above can reduce the number of quatum mechanical overlaps as is clear from Figures 9 and 10. We will list only a few.

(a) For instance, in view of Example
3.6.6
(vi) (b)(
\beta
) and
(\gamma)
,
\text{tr}(P_{8,j}P_{6,k})=0
for
j=0,1,3,4,~k=0,3
and
\text{tr}(P_{8,j}P_{6,k})=0,~\text{for}~j,k=4~\text{or}~1
.

(b) On the other hand, we have
\text{tr}(P_{8,j}P_{l,k})=0,~\text{for}~l=11,5,~j=0,2,4,~k=1,3,5,~\text{or}~j=1,3,5,~\text{and}~k=0,2,4
.

Remark 3.6.9
.

We see that the key is the table in Figure 6 and then the technique indicated by Figures 7 to 10. Next in line is the freedom
provided by explanation in Remark
3.6.8
above. We can suitably modify all the arguments to suit for
d=2a
with ‘
a
’ an odd
prime if we are able to give a right minimal set of
3(a+1)

{\bf W}
-MASS’s and an analogue for the table in Figure 6 and display
the technique by analogues of Tables in Figures 7 to 10.

Same is true for
d=pa
with ‘
p
’ and ‘
a
’ both distinct odd primes with
p<a
, say. In this certain arguments will be easier because
now
d-1
is even and, therefore, by Proposition
3.5.1
and Corollory
3.5.2
,
U_{(m,n)}^{d}=I_{d}
and the set of eigenvalues of
U_{(m,n)}
is

\{\eta^{s}_{d}:s=0,1,\cdots,d-1\}
for any generator
(m,n)
of a full size
\lambda_{(m,n)}
. This will impact the eigenvalues for

U_{(m,n)}^{p}
and
U_{(m,n)}^{a}
to be
\{\eta^{s}_{a}:s=0,1,\cdots a-1\}
and
\{\eta^{t}_{p}:t=0,1,\cdots p-1\}
each of multiplicity
p

and
a
respectively, as well.

3.6.3.
The Case
d=2a
with
a
prime

As already indicated in Remark
3.6.9
above, we will develop analogues of the methods in
§3.6.2 above.

Theorem 3.6.10
.

Simultaneous constraints technique leads to the ideal number
d^{2}
of a complete rank one projection system for
d=2a
with ‘
a
’ an odd prime.

Proof.

We divide the proof in a few parts.

(i)

We begin with the following explicit system
\mathcal{M}
of
{\bf W}
-MASS’s consisting of parts
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2},\mathcal{M}_{3}
given below

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\lambda_{(1,0)},\lambda_{(2j+1,2)},0\leq j\leq a-1\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\lambda_{(2,a)},\lambda_{(2k,1)},0\leq k\leq a-1\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{\lambda_{(1,a)},\lambda_{(2g+1,1)},0\leq g\leq a-1\},

(3.81)

(ii)

We claim that
\mathcal{M}
covers
\mathbb{Z}_{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.

Consider any
(0,0)\neq(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.
If
n=0,m=0
or
m=n
, then
(m,n)=m(1,0)\in\lambda_{(1,0)}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
,
(m,n)=n(0,1)\in\lambda_{(0,1)}\in\mathcal{M}_{2}
and

(m,n)=m(1,1)\in\lambda_{(1,1)}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}
, respectively. Now consider the case
m\neq 0\neq n\neq m
and let

h=\text{HCF}(m,n)
. Then
m=hm^{\prime}
and
n=hn^{\prime}
for
m^{\prime},n^{\prime}
with
\text{HCF}(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})=1
. So
(m,n)=h(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. Thus it is enough to consider the case when
\text{HCF}(m,n)=1
.

(a)

Suppose
n
is even. Then
m
is odd. So
1\leq m\leq 2a-1
.

Now
n=2^{v}k
where
v\geq 1
and
k
is odd. So
1\leq 2^{v-1}k\leq a-1
. Set

k_{1}=k
if
v=1
and
2^{v-1}k+a
if
v\geq 2
. So
\text{HCF}(k_{1},2a)=1
. So there exists a unique
k_{2}
with
1\leq k_{2}\leq 2a-1
and

k_{1}k_{2}=1~(\text{mod}~2a)
. Further,
k_{2}
is odd. So
m_{1}=mk_{2}~(\text{mod}~2a)
is odd and
1\leq m_{1}\leq 2a-1
. Now
(m,n)=(m,2\cdot 2^{v-1}k)

with respect to operations in the ring
(\mathbb{Z}_{d},+,\cdot)
, i.e., addition and multiplication (mod
2a
), we have
k_{1}(m_{1},2)=k_{1}(mk_{2}~(\text{mod}~2a),2)

=(mk_{1}k_{2}~(\text{mod}~2a),2k_{1}~(\text{mod}~2a))=(m,n)
. So
(m,n)\in\lambda_{(m_{1},2)}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
.

(b)

Suppose
m
is even. Then
n
is odd. We first consider the case
n=a
. Then for any odd
v
,
nv=a~(\text{mod}~2a)
. We may interchange the
roles of
m
and
n
in (a) above and obtain that
(m,n)=k_{1}(2,a)\in\lambda_{(2,a)}\in\mathcal{M}_{2}
. Now consider the case
n\neq a
. Then
\text{HCF}(n,2a)=1

. So as in (a) above, there is a unique
n\prime
with
1\leq n^{\prime}\leq 2a-1
and
nn^{\prime}=1~(\text{mod}~2a)
.
Let
m_{1}=mn^{\prime}~(\text{mod}~2a)
. Because
m
is even, we have that
m_{1}
is even. So in the ring
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
,
(m,n)=n(m_{1},1)\in\lambda_{(m_{1},1)}\in\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

(c)

Now suppose that both
m
and
n
are odd. If
n=a
, then
(m,n)=m(1,a)\in\lambda_{(1,a)}
, and if
m=a
, then

(m,n)=n(a,1)\in\lambda_{(a,1)}
. Both
\lambda_{(a,1)}
and
\lambda_{(1,a)}
are in
\mathcal{M}_{3}
. Now, if
m\neq a\neq n
, then
let
n^{\prime}
be as in (b) above. Then
(m,n)=n(m_{1},1)
where
m_{1}=mn^{\prime}~(\text{mod}~2a)
is odd. So
(m,n)\in\lambda_{(m_{1},1)}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}
.

Hence
\mathcal{M}
covers
\mathbb{Z}_{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{d}
and therefore ,
\{U_{(m,n)}:\{(m,n)\in\lambda_{\sigma}~\\
\text{for some}~\sigma~\text{in}~\mathcal{M}\}
covers the unitary basis
\{U_{(m,n)}:(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{d}\}
.

(d)

To get an idea, for the case
a=3
, i.e.
d=6
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\{V_{1},V_{4},V_{9},V_{10}\},\mathcal{M}_{2}=\{V_{8},V_{2},V_{5},V_{11}\},\mathcal{M}_{3}=\{V_{6},V_{3},V_{7},V_{12}\}
occurring in the first, second and third column respectively in the Table in Figure 6.

(iii)

We now figure out overlapping members in the
{\bf W}
-MASS’s, arising from
\mathcal{M}
. It will be convenient to write
(m,n)
in place of
U_{(m,n)}
and,
at times, even for
\lambda_{(m,n)}
it generates.

(a)

For
(m,n)
in
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}~\text{or}~\mathcal{M}_{3}
,
a(m,n)
is
(a,0),(0,a)~\text{or}~(a,a)
respectively.

(b)

The situation for multiples by 2 is a bit different and we need suitable alternative generators and reorderings. So we start with noting them
for the listing as given in (i) above for necessary adjustments in item (iv) below.

\displaystyle{M}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(2,0),(4j+2~(\text{mod}~2a),4),0\leq j\leq a-1\}

\displaystyle=\{(2,0),(4j+2,4),0\leq j\leq\frac{a-3}{2},~\text{and}

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~(4j+2-2a,4)=(4(j-\frac{a-1}{2}),4),\frac{a-1}{2}\leq j\leq a-1\}

\displaystyle=\{(2,0),(4v-2,4),1\leq v=j+1\leq\frac{a-1}{2},(4j^{\prime},4),0\leq j^{\prime}\leq\frac{a-1}{2}\},

\displaystyle{M}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(4,0),(4k~(\text{mod}~2a),2),0\leq k\leq a-1\}

\displaystyle=\left\{(4,0),(4k,2),0\leq k\leq\frac{a-1}{2},\right.

\displaystyle\left.(4k-2a,2)=(4(k-\frac{a-1}{2})-2,2),\frac{a+1}{2}\leq k\leq a-1\right\}

\displaystyle=\{(4,0),(4k,2),0\leq k\leq\frac{a-1}{2},(4k^{\prime}-2,2),1\leq k^{\prime}\leq\frac{a-1}{2}\},

\displaystyle{M}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{(2,0),(4g+2,2),0\leq g\leq a-1\}

\displaystyle=\{(2,0),(4v-2,2),1\leq v\leq\frac{a-1}{2},(4g^{\prime},2),0\leq g^{\prime}\leq\frac{a-1}{2}\}

(3.82)

(iv)

For an application of the simultaneous constraint technique we will need alternative generators for the
{\bf W}
-MASS’s set
in (i) above which we shall now figure out. We deal in the ring
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
unless otherwise stated.

(a)

If
1\leq\ell\leq 2a-1
is odd and
\ell\neq a
, then
(\ell m,\ell n)
is also a generator for full-size
\lambda_{(m,n)}
generated
by
(m,n)
. To see this ,
(\ell m,\ell n)\in\lambda_{(m,n)}
and
s(\ell m,\ell n)=(0,0)
if and only if
s\ell(m,n)=(0,0)
. But

\text{HCF}(\ell,2a)=1
. So
s\ell(m,n)=(0,0)
if and only if
s=2a
. So
\lambda_{(\ell m,\ell n)}=\lambda_{(m,n)}
and
(\ell m,\ell n)
also
generates it.

(b)

Let
\mu=\frac{a+1}{2}
, if it is odd and
a+\frac{a+1}{2}
if
\frac{a+1}{2}
is even. Then
\mu
is odd and
\mu\neq a
. Further,

\text{HCF}(\mu,2a)=1
and, therefore,
\{\nu\mu:0\leq\nu\leq 2a-1\}=\{\alpha:0\leq\alpha\leq 2a-1\}
,
\nu
odd corresponds to
\alpha

odd,
\nu
even corresponds to
\alpha
even,
\nu=0,2,4,a
corresponds to
\alpha=0,a+1,2,a
respectively.

(c)

We replace
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2},\mathcal{M}_{3}
by
\lambda
’s generated by
(m,n)
’s or
(\mu m,\mu n)
’s as per our need which is evident in the format
given below in three parts
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{3}^{\prime}
respectively :

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{(1,0),(2\alpha+1,a+1),~0\leq\alpha\leq a-1\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{(a+1,a),(2k,1),~0\leq k\leq a-1\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{(1,a),(2g+1,1),~0\leq g\leq a-1\}.

(3.83)

Then
a(m,n)=(a,0),(0,a)~\text{or}~(a,a)
for
(m,n)\in\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{3}^{\prime}
respectively.
On the other hand, multiplication
by 2 gives us

\displaystyle{M}_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{(2,0),(4\alpha+2,2),~0\leq\alpha\leq a-1\},

\displaystyle{M}_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{(2,0),(4k,2),~0\leq k\leq a-1\},

\displaystyle{M}_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{(2,0),(4g+2,2),~0\leq g\leq a-1\}.

(3.84)

So some reordering is called for. We prefer to first display it in the Table in Figure 11 in a more pleasing order for constraints

\displaystyle=\left\{(1,0),(a,a+1);(1,a+1),(a+2,a+1),\cdots,\right.

\displaystyle\left.(2b-1,a+1),(a+2b,a+1),\cdots,(a-2,a+1),(2a-1,a+1)\right\},

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\left.\cdots;(2b-1,1),(a+2b,1),\cdots,(a-2,1),(2a-1,1)\right\}.

(3.85)

Then
a(m,n)=(a,0),(0,a),~\text{or}~(a,a)
according as
(m,n)\in\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\prime\prime},~\text{or}~\mathcal{M}_{3}^{\prime\prime}
. Also

(2t,2)

.

(iv)

We are now in a position to modify the proof of Example
3.6.6
and Theorem
3.6.7
by replacing

’ and making other

(a)

Just as in the case of

, we run along the diagonal and then along the first column.That is, we label the
{\bf W}
-MASs’s generated by

,

,

,

(b)

By Proposition
3.5.1
(iii) and Corollary

all these unitaries in (a) above have simple eigenvalues which are

(d)

We choose complete system of unit eigenvectors
\{\xi_{2,k}=\sum_{s=0}^{2a-1}e^{\left(-\frac{ks\pi i}{a}\right)}|s\rangle:0\leq k\leq 2a-1\}

for

(ii) and then the corresponding
system
\mathcal{P}_{2}=\{P_{2,k}=|\xi_{2,k}\rangle\langle\xi_{2,k}|:0\leq k\leq 2a-1\}
of rank one projections. We may use the trivial constraint

\{I_{2a}\}
provided by
\mathcal{P}_{1}
to reduce
\mathcal{P}_{2}
to
\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}
by taking anyone, say, the last one

P_{2,2a-1}
out. Furthermore, just as in (c) above,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
provides constraints
{\bf Q}_{2}
and
{\bf R}_{2}
in an analogous manner.

(e)

The situation for
U_{(1,1)}
or, for that matter,
U_{(m,n)}
for any
(m,n)
in the third column in Figure 9 foliates into two cases as follows
because of the fact that
mn
is odd.

(\alpha)

a+1
is a multiple of
4
, i.e.
a=4v-1
for some
v\geq 1
, and

(
\beta)
)
a-1
is a multiple of
4
, i.e.
a=4v+1
for some
v\geq 1
.

This is because, by Proposition
3.5.1
and the fact that
mn
is odd, we have

\displaystyle U_{(am,an)}=

\displaystyle e^{\left(-\frac{\pi i}{a}\frac{a(a-1)}{2}mn\right)}U_{(m,n)}^{a}

\displaystyle e^{\left(-\frac{\pi i}{2}mn(a-1)\right)}U_{(m,n)}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pm U_{(m,n)}^{a},

(3.86)

according as
a-1
or
a+1
is a multiple of 4.

Now the eigenvalues of
U_{(m,n)}
are
e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{2a}\right)}\{e^{\left(\frac{\pi ik}{a}\right)}:0\leq k\leq 2a-1\}
. So eigenvalues
of
U_{(m,n)}^{a}
are
i
or
-i
corresponding to
k
even or odd respectively.
Thus, the eigenvalues
of
U_{(am,an)}
are
-i
or
i
corresponding to
k
even and odd respectively in case
(\alpha)
( in analogy with the case
d=6
i.e.,
a=3
) but the
eigenvalues of
U_{(am,an)}
are
i
and
-i
corresponding to
k
even and odd respectively in case
(\beta)
, which is new.

On the other hand, the analogy for
U_{(2m,2n)}
with the case
d=6
i.e.,
a=3
, continues in the sense that the eigenvalues for

U_{(m,n)}^{2}
are listed as
e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{a}\right)}\{e^{\left(\frac{2\pi ik}{a}\right)}:0\leq k\leq 2a-1\}

and therefore for
U_{(2m,2n)}
are listed as
e^{\left(2(k-\frac{mn-1}{2})\frac{\pi i}{a}\right)},0\leq k\leq 2a-1
.

In this listing, the values for
k
and
k+a
coincide for
0\leq k\leq a-1
.

In particular, for
U_{(2,2)}
i.e., for
(m,n)=(1,1)
the listing is the usual one as for
U_{(2,0)}
or
U_{(0,2)}
in (b) and (c)
respectively above. But for other
(m,n)
’s, a cyclic permutation may be required.

(f)

In view of (e) above we can go ahead with the analogy with Example

\{\xi_{3,k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2a}}\sum_{s=0}^{2a-1}e^{\left(-\frac{(2k+1)s}{2a}\pi i\right)}e^{\left(\frac{\pi i}{a}\frac{s(s-1)}{2}\right)}|s\rangle,0\leq k\leq 2a-1\}
of unit eigenvectors with corresponding eigenvalues as listed in (e) above and

\mathcal{P}_{3}=\{P_{3k}=|\xi_{3,k}\rangle\langle\xi_{3,k}|:0\leq k\leq 2a-1\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\prime}\cup\cup_{t=2}^{a-1}\mathcal{P}_{t+2}^{\prime}\cup\cup_{j=a+2}^{3a+3}\mathcal{P}_{j}^{\prime}
